      minhash: Y6zE/VL+MKNaFdUyRRzKzAHIa+FQQ+AdvsXIy845zBXNtWBiXSAL5WSZbRTrH1jruN/8umclW3USQLuabbcVUigQe+HxevD42jkaSrPZh3zBZGksHl8NUgrV1oNPrHfcRo8fqPblG+Ry9w/HFBAt7vkUWUkFzaSUH1xFa3eocpvVTBVZvG3ZkQE/fqtIprKDDzGQajSiXvN3XLBhLNwe0Ev1cdnWEfpoVRWheqd819EXiooMhw5DFkdcYGpMfqxH4yJ/ojpn+IY9N7w/eiY2bHtrd08LqOUWpr35Z0ZGmltGW2IdcPl7BCtPnX47k5itaF3QXNfHEwdK6frO/9FmrQ==
      census: 2609.35102v1#env-79
      extractor: ax/v0.5.32
---
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{main result zeros}
    Let $G_0$ be a starting graph with $k$ labeled vertices, and let $\mathcal{R}$ be a recursion operator defining a sequence of graphs $(G_n)_{n\geq 0}$. Suppose that the graph sequence satisfies the following two conditions:
    \begin{enumerate}[label={\normalfont (\roman*)}]
        \item\label{item: non-deg intro} the maximal vertex degrees of the graphs $G_n$ are uniformly bounded, and 
        \item\label{item: exp intro} the minimal distance between pairs of distinct labeled vertices in $G_n$ diverges as $n \rightarrow \infty$. 
    \end{enumerate}
    Then the zeros of the independence polynomials $Z_{G_n}$ avoid a uniform neighborhood of $\mathbb R_{\geq 0}$.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
Let $(G_n)_{n\geq 0}$ be a sequence of graphs as in Theorem~\ref{main result zeros}. Then the limiting free energy per site 
    $$
\rho(\lambda) = \lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log Z_{G_n}(\lambda)}{\#V(G_n)}
$$
    is well defined and real-analytic on all of $\mathbb R_+$, that is, there are no phase transitions for the hard-core model on $(G_n)_{n\geq 0}$.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm: dynamics}
Let $k\in \mathbb N$ and $\lambda\in \mathbb{C}^*$, and suppose $F_\lambda$ is the renormalization
map associated with a graph recursion operator $\mathcal{R}$ on $\mathcal{G}_k$. There exists an explicit $k$-dimensional algebraic variety $\mathcal{M} \subset \mathbb{P}^{2^k-1}$ that is invariant under $F_\lambda$ and such that the following statements are true: 
\begin{enumerate}[label={\normalfont (\roman*)}]
    \item\label{item: dyn1} If $\mathcal{R}$ is non-degenerate, then, after passing to an iterate of $F_\lambda$ if necessary, $F_\lambda$ retracts $\mathcal{M}$ to a subvariety $\mathcal{M}_0$ of dimension $k_0 \le k$, which consists entirely of fixed points.
    \item\label{item: dyn2} If $\mathcal{R}$ is non-degenerate and expanding, then the variety $\mathcal{M}_0$ is transversally superattracting. 
    \item\label{item: dyn3} If $\mathcal{R}$ is non-degenerate and expanding, then for every starting graph $G_0\in \mathcal{G}_k$ and every parameter $\lambda\in \mathbb R_+$, the orbit of the point $\xi_0 = \varphi_\lambda(G_0)$ converges to $\mathcal{M}$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{main bounded zeros}
    Let $k\geq 2$ and $\mathcal{R}$ be a non-degenerate and expanding graph recursion operator on $\mathcal{G}_k$. Consider a recursive graph sequence $(G_n)_{n\geq 0}$ generated by $\mathcal{R}$, that is, $G_{n+1}=\mathcal{R}(G_n)$. If the starting graph $G_0\in \mathcal{G}_k$ is maximally independent, then the zeros of the independence polynomials $Z_{G_n}$ are uniformly bounded and avoid a uniform cone around $\mathbb R_{\geq0}$.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
Let $(G_n)_{n\geq 0}$ be a sequence of finite graphs, and assume that the free energy per site $\rho(\lambda)$ is well-defined for real parameters $\lambda$ in a neighborhood of a fixed parameter $\lambda_0 \ge 0$. Assume moreover that there exists a complex neighborhood $U$ of $\lambda_0$ where all of the partition functions $Z_{G_n}$ are non-zero. Then $\rho$ is real-analytic near $\lambda_0$.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
Since each partition function $Z_{G_n}$ is assumed to be non-zero on $U\subset \mathbb{C}$, the functions
    $$
    \rho_n(\lambda) = \frac{\log Z_{G_n}(\lambda)}{\#V(G_n)}
    $$
    are all well-defined holomorphic functions on $U$. Note that
    $$
    \mathrm{Re} (\rho_n(\lambda)) = \frac{\log |Z_{G_n}(\lambda)|}{\#V(G_n)} \le \frac{\log Z_{G_n}(|\lambda|)}{\#V(G_n)},
    $$
    where the latter holds since the polynomials $Z_{G_n}$ all have non-negative real coefficients. 
    
    Observe that removing edges from a finite graph $G$ can only increase the number of independent sets of $G$ of any fixed size. Hence, for a fixed $\#V(G)$, the maximal value of $Z_{G}(|\lambda|)$ is obtained when the graph $G$ has no edges at all, that is, $Z_G(|\lambda|)\leq (1+|\lambda|)^{\#V(G)}$. It therefore follows that 
    $$
    \mathrm{Re} (\rho_n(\lambda)) \le \frac{\log \left( (1+ |\lambda|)^{\#V(G_n)}\right)}{\#V(G_n)} = \log (1+ |\lambda|).
    $$
    By Montel's fundamental normality test, this implies that the holomorphic functions $\rho_n$ form a normal family on $U$, i.e., every sequence of these functions has a subsequence that converges uniformly on compact subsets of $U$. Since the free energies per site $\rho_n$ are assumed to converge to $\rho$ on the real axis near $\lambda_0$, they must converge on all of $U$ by the Identity Principle. The corresponding limiting map is holomorphic and thus real-analytic on $U\cap \mathbb R$. This implies the desired statement.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def: finite order of ramification}
    Let $G$ be a countably infinite connected graph. We say that $G$ has \emph{finite order of ramification} if there exists $s\in \mathbb N$ such that for every finite connected subgraph $H$ of $G$ there exists a subset $S \subset V(G)$ of cardinality at most $s$ such that every infinite (self-avoiding) path in $G$ that starts in a vertex of $H$ must pass through a vertex of $S$.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm: unique_Gibbs_measure}
 Let $\lambda\in \mathbb R_{\geq 0}$ and $G$ be a countably infinite, locally finite connected graph. Consider the site percolation process on $G$ under which each vertex $v\in V(G)$ is open, independently of the others, with probability $\lambda/(\lambda+1)$ and closed with probability $1/(\lambda+1)$. We denote by $\mathbb{P}_{\lambda/(\lambda+1)}$ the induced probability measure on $2^{V(G)}$, and by $\{\exists\;\text{infinite open path}\}$ the event that there exists an infinite path in $G$ with all of its vertices open. 

 If $\mathbb{P}_{\lambda/(\lambda+1)}[\{\exists\;\text{infinite open path}\}]=0$, then the hard-core model on $G$ with the activity parameter $\lambda$ has a unique Gibbs measure.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
If $G$ is a countably infinite, locally finite connected graph with finite order of ramification, then the hard-core model on $G$ has a unique Gibbs measure for all parameters $\lambda\in \mathbb R_{\geq 0}$.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- example-1: body_md -->
Consider an arbitrary sequence $(H_j)_{j\geq 0}$ of finite connected graphs with $\#V(H_j)\to \infty$ as $j\to \infty$ and for which the limiting free energy per site exists for all $\lambda\in \mathbb R_+$ but is not real-analytic at some positive parameter $\lambda_0$. As an example we can consider the sequence of $d$-ary rooted trees of depth $j$; see also \cite[Proposition~2.7] {SlySun}. Fix a vertex $v(j)\in V(H_j)$ for each $j\geq 0$.  Given an increasing sequence $(j_n)_{n\geq 0}$ of positive integers, we construct recursively a sequence $(G_n)_{n\geq 0}$ of finite graphs as follows: we  set $G_0 = H_{j_0}$ and define $G_{n+1}$ to be the graph obtained from the disjoint union of the graphs $G_n$ and $H_{j_{n+1}}$ by connecting the vertices $v(j_n)$ of $G_n$ and $v(j_{n+1})$ of $H_{j_{n+1}}$ by an edge. The corresponding infinite graph $G$ is then obtained from the disjoint union $\bigsqcup_{n\geq 0} H_{j_{n}}$ by adding an edge between $v(j_{n})$ and $v(j_{n+1})$ for all $n\geq 0$. It is immediate that $G$ is a locally finite connected graph with finite order of ramification.

One also easily checks that, for each fixed $\lambda\in \mathbb R_+$ and $n\geq 0$, we have
\[Z_{G_n}(\lambda)\leq  \prod_{i=0}^{n}Z_{H_{j_i}}(\lambda)\leq Z_{G_n}(\lambda) \cdot (1+\lambda)^{n+1},\]
and thus 
\[\log Z_{G_n}(\lambda)= \sum_{i=0}^{n}\log Z_{H_{j_i}}(\lambda) + O(n).\]
It follows that
\[\frac{\log Z_{G_n}(\lambda)}{\#V(G_n)}= \sum_{i=0}^{n}\frac{\log Z_{H_{j_i}}(\lambda)}{\#V(H_{j_i})} \cdot \frac{\#V(H_{j_i})}{\#V(G_n)} + O\left(\frac{n}{\#V(G_n)}\right),\]
where 
\[\#V(G_n)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\#V(H_{j_i}).\]
Using the Silverman--Toeplitz theorem, we conclude that
\[
\lim_{n\to \infty}\frac{\log Z_{G_n}(\lambda)}{\#V(G_n)}=\lim_{n\to \infty}\frac{\log Z_{H_{j_n}}(\lambda)}{\#V(H_{j_n})},
\]
provided that the sequence $(j_n)_{n\geq 0}$ increases sufficiently fast so that $n/\#V(G_n)\to 0$ as $n\to \infty$. In other words, the limiting free energy per site for the sequence $(G_n)_{n\geq 0}$ exists and coincides with the limiting free energy per site for the sequence $(H_j)_{j\geq 0}$, and in particular is not real-analytic at $\lambda_0$.
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- question-1: body_md -->
Under which conditions on the graph recursion $\mathcal{R}$ and the starting graph $G_0$ does the sequence of probability measures $\nu_n$ defined by the normalized sums 
    \[
    \nu_n = \frac{1}{\deg(Z_{G_n})} \sum_{\lambda\in \mathbb{C}: \; Z_{G_n}(\lambda) = 0} \delta_\lambda
    \]
    of the Dirac masses $\delta_\lambda$ at the zeros of $Z_{G_n}$ (counted with multiplicity) 
    converge weakly to a probability measure $\mu$? If the limiting measure $\mu$ exists, how do its properties depend on the graph recursion and starting graph?
<!-- end question-1 -->
<!-- question-2: body_md -->
Let $F_\lambda: \mathbb{P}^N \dashrightarrow \mathbb{P}^N$ be a one-parameter family of rational maps, let $\varphi: \mathbb{C} \rightarrow \mathbb{P}^N$ be a  rational map, and let $\Sigma: \mathbb{C}^{N+1} \rightarrow \mathbb{C}$ be a homogeneous polynomial. Under which conditions on $F_\lambda$, $\varphi$, and $\Sigma$, do the probability measures $\nu_n$, given by the normalized sums of the Dirac masses at the solutions of the equation
    \[
    (\Sigma \circ F_\lambda^n \circ \varphi)(\lambda) = 0,
    \]
    equidistribute toward a probability measure $\mu$?
<!-- end question-2 -->
<!-- question-3: body_md -->
Do the main results in this paper hold when allowing arbitrary connecting graphs? In particular, do the zeros of the independence polynomials $Z_{G_n}$ still avoid a uniform neighborhood of $\mathbb R_{\geq 0}$ for non-degenerate and expanding graph recursion operators $\mathcal{R}$ in this framework?
<!-- end question-3 -->
<!-- question-4: body_md -->
Is it possible to characterize for which recursion operators $\mathcal{R}\colon \mathcal{G}_k\to \mathcal{G}_k$ as in the more general framework from \cite{HP2024}, the hard-core model on the recursive sequences $(G_n)_{n\geq 0}$, $G_{n+1}=\mathcal{R}(G_n)$, has a phase transition?
<!-- end question-4 -->
<!-- question-5: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{R}$ be a non-degenerate and expanding graph recursion operator, and consider the sequence $(G_n)_{n \ge 0}$, $G_{n+1}=\mathcal{R}(G_n)$, obtained from a starting graph $G_0$. Is it true that, in the ferromagnetic Ising model, for all fixed $b\in(0,1)$ the Lee--Yang zeros of $Z_{G_n}$ avoid a uniform neighborhood of $\lambda=1$?
<!-- end question-5 -->
<!-- question-6: body_md -->
Suppose that each of the recursion operators $\mathcal{R}_i$ is expanding and non-degenerate. Does it follow that the zeros of the independence polynomials $Z_{G_n}$ almost surely avoid a uniform neighborhood of $\mathbb R_{\geq 0}$?
<!-- end question-6 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem: Dobrushin contraction}
    Suppose $A$ and $B$ are two stochastic matrices whose dimensions are such that the product $AB$ is well-defined. Then 
    $\delta(AB)\leq \delta(A)\delta(B)$.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
It is sufficient to consider the case when the matrix $B$, and hence also $C:=AB$, has only two columns. We denote the number of rows and columns of $A$ by $r$ and $s$, respectively. Let us also denote the columns of $A$ by $A_1,\dots, A_s$, the columns of $C$ by $C_1$ and $C_2$, and the entries of $B$ by $b_{i,j}$. We then aim to obtain an upper estimate for
    \[
    \|C_1-C_2\|_1 = \|\sum_{j=1}^s (b_{j,1} - b_{j,2})A_j  \|_1.
    \]    
    If $\delta(B)=0$, then $\delta(C)=0$ and the statement trivially holds. So we assume in the following that $\delta(B)>0$. 
     
     We may renumber the row indices of $B$ and correspondingly the column indices of $A$ so that
     \[\text{$b_{j,1} \ge b_{j,2}$\;
     for $j = 1 ,\ldots ,j_0$ \quad and \quad 
     $b_{j,1} < b_{j,2}$ \; for $j = j_0 + 1 ,\ldots, s$.}\] 
     Since 
    \[
    1=\sum_{j=1}^s b_{j,1}=\sum_{j=1}^{s}b_{j,2}
    \]
    and
    \[
    2\delta(B)=\sum_{j=1}^s|b_{j,1}-b_{j,2}|=\sum_{j=1}^{j_0}(b_{j,1}-b_{j,2})+\sum_{j=j_0+1}^{s}(b_{j,2}-b_{j,1}),
    \]
    it follows that 
    \[
     \delta(B)=\sum_{j=1}^{j_0}(b_{j,1}-b_{j,2})=\sum_{j=j_0+1}^{s}(b_{j,2}-b_{j,1}).
    \] 

    Let us set 
    \[\text{$x_j:=\frac{1}{\delta(B)}(b_{j,1}-b_{j,2})$\;
     for $j = 1 ,\ldots ,j_0$ \quad and \quad 
    $y_i:=\frac{1}{\delta(B)}(b_{i,2}-b_{i,1})$ \; for $i = j_0 + 1 ,\ldots, s$},\]
    so that
    \[1=\sum_{j=1}^{j_0}x_j=\sum_{i=j_0+1}^{s}y_i.\]
    Then 
    \begin{align*}
     \frac{1}{\delta(B)}\|C_1-C_2\|_1 = &\|\sum_{j=1}^{j_0} x_jA_j- \sum_{i=j_0+1}^s y_iA_i \|_1=\|\sum_{\substack{{j=1,\dots,j_0,}\\{i=j_0+1,\dots, s}}}x_jy_i(A_j-A_i) \|_1\leq\\
     &\sum_{\substack{{j=1,\dots,j_0,}\\{i=j_0+1,\dots, s}}}x_jy_i\|A_j-A_i\|_1 \leq \sum_{\substack{{j=1,\dots,j_0,}\\{i=j_0+1,\dots, s}}}x_jy_i 2\delta(A) = 2\delta(A), 
    \end{align*}
    which establishes the desired inequality $\delta(C)\leq \delta(A)\delta(B)$.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem: probability lower bound} 
    Given $\lambda \in \mathbb R_+$ and $n, \Delta \in \mathbb N$, there exists a constant $c(\lambda, \Delta, n)>0$ such that the following holds: 
    
    For every graph $G$ and every non-empty subset $X\subset V(G)$ whose vertices have degree at most $\Delta$, we have
    \[
    \mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\tau] \geq c(\lambda, \Delta, \#X)
    \]
    for all admissible assignments $\tau$ on $X$.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Consider the subset $Y:=\bigcup_{v\in X} N_G[v]$. We can then write
    \[
    \mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\tau] = \sum_{\sigma} \mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\tau : \sigma] \mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\sigma],
    \]
    where we sum over all possible admissible assignments $\sigma$ on $V(G) \setminus Y$. Observe now that for the independence polynomial, considering independent sets in $G$ that agree with an assignment $\sigma$ on $V(G) \setminus Y$ is equivalent to considering independent sets in an induced subgraph $G[S]$ for a certain subset $S=S(\sigma)$ of $Y$. More precisely, let $S \subset Y$ consist of those vertices in $Y$ that are not adjacent to any vertex in $\sigma^{-1}(1)$. Then $X \subset S$ and 
    \[
    \mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\tau : \sigma] = \mathbb{P}_{G[S],\lambda}[\tau].
    \]
   
    Since 
    \[Z^\tau_{G[S]}(\lambda) \geq \lambda^{\#\tau^{-1}(1)}\geq \min\{1,\lambda^{\#X}\} \quad \text{and} \quad Z_{G[S]}(\lambda) \leq (1+\lambda)^{\#S}\leq (1+\lambda)^{\#X(\Delta+1)},\]
    we get that 
    \[\mathbb{P}_{G[S],\lambda}[\tau]\geq c := \frac{ \min\{1,\lambda^{\#X}\}}{(1+\lambda)^{\#X(\Delta+1)}}>0,\]
    and thus
    \[
    \mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\tau] = \sum_{\sigma} \mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\tau : \sigma] \mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\sigma] \geq   \sum_{\sigma} c  \, \mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\sigma] = c,
    \]
    with $c=c(\lambda,\Delta,\#X)$ as desired. This finishes the proof.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem: conditioning wrt Y}
    Let $G$ be a graph, and let $\tau$, $\eta$, and $\sigma$ be vertex assignments on pairwise disjoint subsets $X\neq \emptyset$, $Y$, and $W\neq \emptyset$ of $V(G)$, respectively.  Suppose that every path from a vertex in $X$ to a vertex in $W$ passes through a vertex in $Y$. Then, for all $\lambda\in \mathbb R_+$, we have
    \[\mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\tau: \eta\wedge \sigma] = \mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\tau: \eta],\]
    provided that $\mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\eta\wedge \sigma]\neq 0$.
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Let $H$ be the induced subgraph of $G$ that contains $X\sqcup Y$ as well as all vertices of $G$ that can be reached by a path starting in $X$ and not passing through any vertex of $Y$. Note that then $W\subset V(G) \setminus V(H)$. The desired statement will follow directly from the following claim using the law of total probability.

    \begin{claim}
    Suppose $\widetilde{\sigma}$ is an arbitrary assignment on $V(G) \setminus V(H)$ such that $\eta\wedge \widetilde{\sigma}$ is admissible for $G$. Then 
    \[\mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\tau: \eta\wedge \widetilde{\sigma}] = \mathbb{P}_{H,\lambda}[\tau: \eta] = \mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\tau: \eta].\]    
    \end{claim}
    
    Indeed, we have 
    \[
    Z_{G}^{\tau\wedge \eta\wedge \widetilde{\sigma}}(\lambda)=Z_{H}^{\tau\wedge \eta}(\lambda)\cdot \lambda^{\#\widetilde{\sigma}^{-1}(1)} \quad \text{and} \quad 
    Z_{G}^{\eta\wedge \widetilde{\sigma}}(\lambda)=Z_{H}^{\eta}(\lambda)\cdot \lambda^{\#\widetilde{\sigma}^{-1}(1)}, 
    \]
    which imply the first equality. The second equality follows immediately from the following chain of identities (based on the law of total probability):
    \begin{align*}
    \mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\tau: \eta]&=\sum_{\widetilde{\sigma}}\mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\tau\wedge \widetilde{\sigma}: \eta] = \sum_{\widetilde{\sigma}}\mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\tau: \widetilde{\sigma} \wedge \eta] \, \mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\widetilde{\sigma}: \eta]  \\
    &=\sum_{\widetilde{\sigma}}\,\mathbb{P}_{H,\lambda}[\tau:\eta] \mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\widetilde{\sigma}: \eta] = \mathbb{P}_{H,\lambda}[\tau:\eta] \cdot \sum_{\widetilde{\sigma}}\mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\widetilde{\sigma}: \eta] \\
    &= \mathbb{P}_{H,\lambda}[\tau:\eta],
    \end{align*}
    where all the sums are taken over all possible vertex assignments $\widetilde{\sigma}$ on $V(G) \setminus V(H)$ such that $\widetilde{\sigma}\wedge \eta$ is admissible for $G$.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- claim-1: body_md -->
Suppose $\widetilde{\sigma}$ is an arbitrary assignment on $V(G) \setminus V(H)$ such that $\eta\wedge \widetilde{\sigma}$ is admissible for $G$. Then 
    \[\mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\tau: \eta\wedge \widetilde{\sigma}] = \mathbb{P}_{H,\lambda}[\tau: \eta] = \mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\tau: \eta].\]
<!-- end claim-1 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem: total var bound} 
    Given $\lambda \in \mathbb R_+$ and $n \in \mathbb N$, there exists a constant $\varepsilon(\lambda, n)\in (0,1)$ such that the following holds: 
    
    For every graph $G$, for all disjoint non-empty subsets $X,W\subset V(G)$, and for all admissible assignments $\sigma,\widetilde{\sigma}$ on $W$, we have
    \[\frac{1}{2}\; \sum_{\tau: X\to \{0,1\}} |\mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\tau : \sigma]-\mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\tau : \widetilde\sigma]| \leq \varepsilon(\lambda, \#X),\]
    where the sum is taken over all assignments $\tau$ on $X$.
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Recall that ${\bf 0}_X$ denotes the constant assignment on $X$ that assigns the value $0$ to each vertex. 
Then, for every assignment $\tau$ on $X$ and every admissible assignment $\sigma$ on $W$, we have 
    \[
    Z^{\tau\wedge\sigma}_{G}(\lambda)\leq \lambda^{\#\tau^{-1}(1)}\cdot Z^{{\bf 0}_X\wedge\sigma}_{G}(\lambda)\leq \max\{1,\lambda^{\#X}\}\cdot Z^{{\bf 0}_X\wedge\sigma}_{G}(\lambda),
    \]
   and thus
   \[
   \mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[{\bf 0}_X:\sigma]=   \frac{Z^{{\bf 0}_X\wedge\sigma}_{G}(\lambda)}{Z^{\sigma}_{G}(\lambda)} = \frac{Z^{{\bf 0}_X\wedge\sigma}_{G}(\lambda)}{\sum_{\tau}Z^{\tau\wedge\sigma}_{G}(\lambda)} \geq \frac{1}{2^{\#X}\max\{1,\lambda^{\#X}\}},
   \]
   where the sum is taken over all possible assignments $\tau$ on $X$. 

   Suppose now that  $\sigma,\widetilde{\sigma}$ are arbitrary admissible assignments on $W$. Since $|a-b|=a+b-2\min\{a,b\}$, we obtain
   \begin{align*} 
   &\sum_{\tau: X\to \{0,1\}} |\mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\tau : \sigma]-\mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\tau : \widetilde{\sigma}]|=\\
   &\sum_{\tau: X\to \{0,1\}} \mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\tau : \sigma]+\sum_{\tau: X\to \{0,1\}} \mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\tau : \widetilde{\sigma}]-2\sum_{\tau: X\to \{0,1\}}\min\{\mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\tau :  \sigma],\, \mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[\tau : \widetilde{\sigma}]\}\leq\\
   &1+1-2\min\{\mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[{\bf 0}_X :  \sigma],\, \mathbb{P}_{G,\lambda}[{\bf 0}_X : \widetilde{\sigma}]\}\leq 2\left(1-\frac{1}{2^{\#X}\max\{1,\lambda^{\#X}\}}\right),
   \end{align*}
    which implies the desired statement with
    \[
    \varepsilon(\lambda,n):=1-\frac{1}{2^{n}\max\{1,\lambda^{n}\}}\in (0,1).
    \]
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def:graph recursion} Let $(H, \Phi)$ be a gluing data with parameters $k,m\in \mathbb N$ as above, and suppose that we are given a graph $G\in\mathcal{G}_k$. We define a new marked graph $\mathcal{R}(G)=\mathcal{R}_{(H,\Phi)}(G)\in \mathcal{G}_k$ as follows:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\Roman*)]
    \item First, we take $m$ disjoint copies $G(1),\dots, G(m)$ of $G$, where each copy $G(i)$ is associated with the vertex $i$ of the gluing scheme $H$.

    \item Then for each edge $e = \{i_1, \ldots, i_s\} \in E(H)$ with label $\ell(e)\in\{1,\dots,k\}$, we identify the vertices labeled $\ell(e)$ in the copies $G(i_1),\dots,G(i_s)$.

    \item Finally, we assign $k$ marked vertices in the resulting graph according to the labeling map $\Phi$: the label $j \in \{1, \ldots, k\}$ is assigned to the vertex that corresponds to the edge $\Phi(j) \in E(H)$.
\end{enumerate}
We call the induced operator $$
\mathcal{R} = \mathcal{R}_{(H, \Phi)}: \mathcal{G}_k \rightarrow \mathcal{G}_k, \; G\mapsto \mathcal{R}(G),
$$
the \emph{graph recursion} with parameters $k,m$ associated with the gluing data $(H,\Phi)$.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- rem-1: body_md -->
\label{rem: general_rec}
    In \cite{HP2024}, we considered more general gluing data and graph recursions. There, roughly speaking, the $m$ copies of the given graph $G\in \mathcal{G}_k$ are joined by some auxiliary \emph{connecting graphs} $\Sigma_e$ for every edge $e\in E(H)$. Additionally, each label $j\in\{1,\dots,k\}$ is assigned to a distinguished marked vertex within the graph $\Sigma_{\Phi(j)}$. The graph recursion considered in this paper corresponds to the special case where each $\Sigma_e$ consists of a single vertex.
<!-- end rem-1 -->
<!-- example-2: body_md -->
\label{ex: sierpinksi}
    We now describe the \emph{Sierpi\'nski gluing data} $(H,\Phi)$ with parameters $k=3$ and $m=3$ governing the recursive construction of the Sierpi\'nski tripod graphs from Figure~\ref{fig: sierpinski tripod graphs} and the Sierpi\'nski gasket graphs from Figure~\ref{fig: sierpinski graphs}. The gluing scheme is given by the hypergraph $H$ with 
    \[V(H)=\{1,2,3\} \quad \text{and} \quad E(H)=\lbrace \{1\},\{2\},\{3\},\{1,2\},\{1,3\},\{2,3\}\rbrace.\]
    For each $j \in \{1,2,3\}$, we label the $1$-edge $\{j\}$ and the $2$-edge $\{1,2,3\}\setminus \{j\}$ with the label $j$. We note that these two edges form a partition of $V(H)=\{1,2,3\}$. The labeling map $\Phi$ is defined by $\Phi(j) = \{j\}$ for each $j\in \{1,2,3\}$. We remark that, since the elements of $E(H)$ are pairwise distinct, an edge of $H$ is already determined by the respective subset of $V(H)$, and thus it is not necessary to specify the label of the edge $\{j\}$.
    When we take the starting graph to be a $3$-star (i.e., a \emph{tripod}) and iterate $\mathcal{R}_{(H,\Phi)}$, we obtain the Sierpi\'nski tripod graphs from Figure~\ref{fig: sierpinski tripod graphs}; when we take the starting graph to be a $3$-cycle, we obtain the Sierpi\'nski gasket graphs from Figure~\ref{fig: sierpinski graphs}.
<!-- end example-2 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{def: label dynamics}
    A gluing data $(H,\Phi)$ with parameters $k,m$ induces a dynamical system $\Lambda=\Lambda_{(H,\Phi)}: \{1,\ldots, k\}\to \{1,\ldots, k\}$ on the labels: namely, we map each label $j\in \{1,\dots, k\}$ to the label $\Lambda(j):=\ell(\Phi(j))$ of the edge $\Phi(j)\in E(H)$. A label $j$ is called \emph{periodic} if $\Lambda^{p}(j)=j$ for some $p\geq 1$. Evidently, every label is eventually mapped to a periodic one under iteration of $\Lambda$.
    
    The number $\#\Phi(j)$ is called the (\emph{local}) \emph{degree} of a label $j$.   
    We say that $j$ is \emph{critical} if $\#\Phi(j)\geq 2$.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
\label{def: non-deg_and_exp} We say that a gluing data $(H,\Phi)$ with parameters $k,m$ and the associated  graph recursion $\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{(H,\Phi)}$ are:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \emph{non-degenerate} if the induced dynamical system $\Lambda=\Lambda_{(H,\Phi)}$ has no periodic critical labels, and \emph{degenerate} in the opposite case;

    \item \emph{expanding} if there exists $n\in \mathbb N$ such that the edges $\Phi_n(1), \ldots, \Phi_n(k)$ form pairwise disjoint subsets of $V(H_n)$, where $(H_n, \Phi_n)$ is the $n$-th iterate of $(H, \Phi)$. 
\end{itemize}
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- example-3: body_md -->
For the Sierpi\'nski gluing data $(H,\Phi)$, we have $\Lambda(j) = j$ for all $j \in \{1,2,3\}$, and each label has degree $1$. In particular, this data is non-degenerate. At the same time, since $\Phi(j)=\{j\}$ for each label $j$, we have that the data is also expanding.
<!-- end example-3 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem: degeneration and expansion}
Let $\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{(H,\Phi)}$ be a graph recursion with parameters $k$ and $m$, and $\Lambda=\Lambda_{(H,\Phi)}$ be the corresponding dynamical system on the labels. Suppose $N\in \mathbb N$ is an iterate such that $\Lambda^N(j)=j$ for each periodic label $j\in\{1,\dots,k\}$ and $\Lambda^{2N}(j)=\Lambda^N(j)$ for each non-periodic label $j$, and let $(H_N, \Phi_N)$ be the gluing data of $\mathcal{R}^N$. Then the following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{itemize}
    \item the recursion $\mathcal{R}$ is non-degenerate;
    \item for each periodic label $j\in\{1,\dots,k\}$, we have $\#\Phi_N(j)=1$;
    \item for every starting graph $G_0 \in \mathcal{G}_k$, the vertex degrees in the graphs $G_n=\mathcal{R}^n(G_0)$ are uniformly bounded (in $n$);
    \item for some starting graph $G_0 \in \mathcal{G}_k$ where each vertex with a periodic label is non-isolated, the vertex degrees in the graphs $G_n=\mathcal{R}^n(G_0)$ are uniformly bounded (in $n$).
\end{itemize}
Similarly, the following conditions are equivalent: 
\begin{itemize}
    \item the recursion $\mathcal{R}$ is expanding;
    \item for each pair of distinct periodic labels $j,j'\in\{1,\dots,k\}$ the corresponding edges $\Phi_N(j)$ and $\Phi_N(j')$ form disjoint subsets of $V(H_N)$;
    \item for every starting graph $G_0 \in \mathcal{G}_k$ and for all distinct labels $j,j'\in\{1,\dots,k\}$ the graph distance between the vertices labeled $j$ and $j'$ in $G_n=\mathcal{R}^n(G_0)$ goes to $\infty$ as $n\to \infty$;
    \item for some starting graph $G_0 \in \mathcal{G}_k$ where all vertices with periodic labels lie in the same connected component, we have that for all distinct periodic labels $j,j'\in\{1,\dots,k\}$ the graph distance between the vertices labeled $j$ and $j'$ in $G_n=\mathcal{R}^n(G_0)$ goes to $\infty$ as $n\to \infty$.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- rem-2: body_md -->
\label{rem: expansion}
    The definitions imply that, if a gluing data $(H,\Phi)$ is expanding, then the edges $\Phi_n(1), \ldots, \Phi_n(k)$ form pairwise disjoint subsets of $V(H_n)$ for  \emph{all} sufficiently large $n\in \mathbb N$. In fact, this holds for all $n \ge N+1$, where $N$ is as in the statement of Lemma~\ref{lem: degeneration and expansion}.
<!-- end rem-2 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem: limit_energy}
    Let $\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{(H,\Phi)}$ be an arbitrary graph recursion with parameters $k, m$, and let $G_0 \in \mathcal{G}_k$ be an arbitrary starting graph. Suppose $(G_n)_{n \ge 0}\subset \mathcal{G}_k$ is the sequence of graphs 
    defined recursively by $G_{n+1} = \mathcal{R}(G_n)$. Then the free energy per site $\rho_n(\lambda)=\frac{\log{Z_{G_n}(\lambda)}}{\# V(G_n)}$ for the sequence $(G_n)_{n\geq 0}$ converges for all $\lambda\in\mathbb R_+$, i.e., the limiting free energy per site $\rho(\lambda) = \lim_{n\to\infty } \rho_n(\lambda)$ is well defined.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
Since $G_{n+1}$ is constructed by taking $m$ copies of $G_n$ and identifying a fixed number of vertices, we have that
    $$
    \# V(G_{n+1}) = m \cdot \# V(G_n) - C,
    $$
    where the constant integer $C\geq 0$ depends only on $\mathcal{R}$ and not on $G_n$. In fact, \[C=\sum_{e\in E(H)} (\#e-1)\leq k(m-1).\] It follows that
    \[
    \alpha_n:=\frac{\# V(G_n)}{m^n}=\#V(G_0)-C\cdot \sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{m^i}
    \]
    converges to 
    \[\alpha:=\#V(G_0)-\frac{C}{m-1}\geq 0  \quad \mathrm{as} \quad n \rightarrow \infty.
    \] 
    We note that $\alpha>0$, unless $k=\#V(G_0)$ and the gluing scheme $H$ has a single edge $\{1,\dots, m\}$ labeled $j$ for each $j\in \{1,\dots, k\}$. In the latter case, the sequence  $(G_n)_{n \ge 0}$ is constant (up to edge multiplicities) and the statement is trivial. Hence, we may assume that $\alpha>0$ in the following. 

    
    Now observe that by identifying some of the vertices in a given graph, the number of independent sets of a fixed size can only decrease. It follows that
    $$
    Z_{G_{n+1}}(\lambda) \le \big(Z_{G_n}(\lambda)\big)^m  \quad \text{for all} \quad \lambda\in \mathbb R_+. 
    $$
    Hence, for each fixed $\lambda\in\mathbb R_+$, the sequence $$
    \beta_n:=\frac{\log Z_{G_n}(\lambda)}{m^n}
    $$
    is decreasing in $n$, and thus converges to some $\beta(\lambda)\geq0$. It follows that the free energy per site $\rho_n(\lambda)=\frac{\beta_n}{\alpha_n}$ converges to $\frac{\beta(\lambda)}{\alpha}$ as $n\to\infty$, which finishes the proof.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- example-4: body_md -->
\label{ex: diamon_lattice}
    For the diamond hierarchical graphs $D_n$ (see Figure~\ref{fig: diamond}), the corresponding gluing data $(H,\Phi)$ has parameters $k=2$ and $m=4$. The gluing scheme $H$ is a $4$-cycle, whose edges are alternately labeled $1$ and $2$, and the labeling map $\Phi$ assigns the labels $1$ and $2$ to the two ``opposite'' edges in $H$ labeled $1$. The resulting recursion $\mathcal{R}_{(H,\Phi)}$ is expanding and degenerate. In fact, the distance between the two marked vertices of $D_n$ is $2^n$, and the vertex degrees of both of these vertices are $2^{n}$ as well.
<!-- end example-4 -->
<!-- example-5: body_md -->
\label{ex: hier_lattices} 
   Further examples of hierarchical lattices were considered in \cite{chio2021chromatic}. Formally, a \emph{hierarchical lattice} is a sequence $(\Gamma_n)_{n\geq 0}$ of graphs in $\mathcal{G}_2$,  constructed recursively in the following way. Start with a single-edge graph $\Gamma_0$ (with two vertices) and fix a graph $\Gamma_1=\Gamma\in \mathcal{G}_2$ that is symmetric with respect to its marked vertices, called the \emph{generating graph}. For each $n\in \mathbb N$, define the graph $\Gamma_{n}$ by replacing each edge of $\Gamma$ with a copy of $\Gamma_{n-1}$, using the two marked vertices in $\Gamma_{n-1}$ as if they were the endpoints of that edge. 
   We also mark the two vertices of $\Gamma_n$ corresponding to the marked vertices of $\Gamma$, preserving their original labels. Equivalently, we may also construct $\Gamma_n$ by replacing each edge of $\Gamma_{n-1}$ with $\Gamma$. It is easy to see that such sequences $(\Gamma_n)_{n\geq 0}$ fit into our framework of recursive graphs whenever the generating graph $\Gamma$ is bipartite, i.e. its vertex set can be partitioned into two independent subsets. In this case, the corresponding gluing data is non-degenerate if and only if both marked vertices of $\Gamma$ have vertex degree $1$. Furthermore, the gluing data is expanding if and only if there is no edge in $\Gamma$ connecting the marked vertices.
<!-- end example-5 -->
<!-- example-6: body_md -->
\label{ex: dendrite}
   Our final example is a graph recursion induced by the dynamics of the quadratic polynomial $z^2+i$ on the complex plane. The corresponding gluing data $(H,\Phi)$ has parameters $k=3$ and $m=2$. The gluing scheme $H$ is a multi-graph with the vertex set $V(H)=\{1,2\}$ and edge multi-set $E(H)$ given by the union of the following three partitions of $V(H)$:
   \[\lbrace\{1,2\}\rbrace, \; \lbrace\{1\},\{2\}\rbrace, \; \text{and }\; \lbrace\{1\},\{2\}\rbrace.\]
   We label the edges in the three partitions above by $1$, $2$, and $3$, respectively. The labeling map $\Phi$ is now defined as follows: we set $\Phi(1)$ to be the edge $\{1\}$ labeled $2$, $\Phi(2)$ to be the edge $\{1\}$ labeled $3$, and $\Phi(3)$ to be the edge $\{2\}$ labeled $2$. Figure~\ref{fig: dendrite} illustrates the iteration of $\mathcal{R}_{(H,\Phi)}$ when we choose the initial graph $T_0$ to be the tripod graph (i.e., a $3$-star) with labeled 
   leaves. The graph $T_0$ corresponds to the \emph{Hubbard tree} of the polynomial $p(z)=z^2+i$ (see, for example, \cite[Definition~4.1]{DH_Orsay} for the definition), while the trees $T_n$ ($n\geq 1$) correspond to the iterated preimages of the Hubbard tree under $p$. We call the gluing data $(H,\Phi)$ the \emph{$(z^2+i)$-gluing data}. Note that $(H,\Phi)$ is non-degenerate and expanding. We remark that, similarly, we may define a gluing data for every \emph{Misiurewicz polynomial}, that is, for a polynomial whose finite critical points are strictly preperiodic. All such gluing data will be non-degenerate and expanding as well.
<!-- end example-6 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:x_prime} Suppose $\lambda\in \mathbb{C}^*$. 
For each ${\bf x}\in \{0,1\}^k$ and $\eta: Y\to \{0,1\}$, 
let 
$$
\|{\bf x} \wedge \eta\|_H  := \sum_e (\#e-1) = \sum_{i=1}^m
\#[{\bf x}\wedge\eta]_i^{-1}(1)- \#({\bf x}\wedge \eta)^{-1}(1),
$$
where the first sum is taken over all edges $e\in E(H)$ that correspond to the vertices of $\mathcal{R}(G)$ receiving value~$1$ from the assignment ${\bf x}\wedge \eta$.  
Then 
\begin{equation}\label{eq: x_and_y_prime_conditioned}
Z_{\mathcal{R}(G)}^{{\bf x}\wedge \eta}(\lambda) = \lambda^{-\|{\bf x}\wedge \eta\|_H} \prod_{i=1}^m Z_{G(i)}^{[{\bf x}\wedge \eta]_i}(\lambda),
\end{equation}
and hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq: x_prime_conditioned}
Z_{\mathcal{R}(G)}^{{\bf x}}(\lambda) =\sum_{\eta: Y\to \{0,1\}} \lambda^{-\|{\bf x}\wedge \eta\|_H} \prod_{i=1}^m Z_{G}^{[{\bf x}\wedge \eta]_i}(\lambda).
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Intuitively, the exponent $\|{\bf x}\wedge\eta\|_H$ in \eqref{eq: x_and_y_prime_conditioned} records the overcounting of vertices receiving a $1$ from the assignment ${\bf x}\wedge\eta$, and which arise from the identifications prescribed by the gluing scheme $H$. To see this formally, let ${\bf x}\in \{0,1\}^k$ and $\eta: Y\to \{0,1\}$ be arbitrary. We assume below that the assignment ${\bf x} \wedge \eta$ is  admissible; for otherwise, both sides of \eqref{eq: x_and_y_prime_conditioned} vanish, and thus it trivially holds. Since only the labeled vertices of the disjoint copies $G(i)$ are getting identified to form the graph $\mathcal{R}(G)$, it follows that removing all vertices in $P(\mathcal{R}(G))\sqcup Y$ decomposes the graph $\mathcal{R}(G)$ into $m$ disjoint copies of the induced graph $G[V(G)\setminus P]$. 

For each $i\in\{1,\dots,m\}$, let $G'(i)$ be the induced subgraph obtained from the copy $G(i)$ by removing every labeled vertex that receives a $0$ from the assignment $[{\bf x} \wedge \eta]_i$, as well as the closed neighborhood of each labeled vertex that receives a $1$ from $[{\bf x} \wedge \eta]_i$. Note that each $G'(i)$ is a subgraph of the respective copy of $G[V(G)\setminus P]$. Properties \ref{prop:ind-poly-1} and \ref{prop:ind-poly-2} then imply that, up to multiplication by a suitable power of $\lambda$, the ${\bf x}\wedge\eta$-conditioned independence polynomial $Z_{\mathcal{R}(G)}^{{\bf x}\wedge \eta}(\lambda)$ is the product of the independence polynomials of the graphs $G'(i)$. More precisely, we have
\[
Z_{\mathcal{R}(G)}^{{\bf x}\wedge \eta}(\lambda) = \lambda^{\#({\bf x}\wedge\eta)^{-1}(1)}\prod_{i=1}^m Z_{G'(i)}(\lambda).
\]
At the same time, for each $i\in \{1,\dots,m\}$ property \ref{prop:ind-poly-2} implies that
\[
Z_{G(i)}^{[{\bf x}\wedge \eta]_i}(\lambda) = \lambda^{\#[{\bf x}\wedge\eta]_i^{-1}(1)} Z_{G'(i)}(\lambda).
\]
Substituting this expression into the previous identity yields \eqref{eq: x_and_y_prime_conditioned}, which in turn implies \eqref{eq: x_prime_conditioned}. This finishes the proof.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{cor: renormalization map}
    Let $\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{(H,\Phi)}$ be the graph recursion operator associated with a gluing data $(H,\Phi)$ with parameters $k,m$. For each fixed parameter $\lambda\in \mathbb{C}^*$, there exists a homogeneous polynomial self-map $\widehat{F}_\lambda:\mathbb{C}^{2^k}\to\mathbb{C}^{2^k}$ of degree $m$ (i.e., every coordinate map of $\widehat{F}_\lambda$ is given by a homogeneous polynomial of degree $m$) such that 
    $$
    \widehat{F}_\lambda(\widehat{\varphi}_\lambda(G)) = \widehat{\varphi}_\lambda(\mathcal{R}(G))
    $$
    for all $G\in \mathcal{G}_k$. Hence, $\widehat{F}_\lambda$ induces a rational self-map $F_\lambda: \mathbb{P}^{2^k-1}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2^k-1}$ of degree $m$ such that 
    $$
    F_\lambda(\varphi_\lambda(G)) = \varphi_\lambda(\mathcal{R}(G))
    $$
    for all $G\in \mathcal{G}_k$ for which these values are well-defined. Moreover, the following diagram commutes:
    \begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[->,>={Stealth[round]},shorten >=1pt,auto,semithick]
  \node (G1) {$\mathcal{G}_k$};
  \node (G2) [right=2cm of G1] {$\mathcal{G}_k$};
  \node (C1) [below=2cm of G1] {$\mathbb{C}^{2^k}$};
  \node (C2) [below=2cm of G2] {$\mathbb{C}^{2^k}$};
  \draw[->] (G1) to node {$\mathcal{R}$} (G2);
  \draw[->] (C1) to node {$\widehat{F}_\lambda$} (C2);

  \draw[->] (G1) to node {$\widehat{\varphi}_\lambda$} (C1);
  \draw[->] (G2) to node {$\widehat{\varphi}_\lambda$} (C2);

   \node (P1) [below=2cm of C1] {$\mathbb{P}^{2^k-1}$};
  \node (P2) [below=2cm of C2] {$\mathbb{P}^{2^k-1}$.};
    \draw[->, dashed] (P1) to node {$F_\lambda$} (P2);
  \draw[->,dashed] (C1) to node {} (P1);
  \draw[->,dashed] (C2) to node {} (P2);

  \node (Z0) [below=1cm of G2] {};
  \node (Z) [right=2cm of Z0] {$\mathbb{C}$};
    \draw[->,-latex] (G2)--(Z) node[midway,above,sloped] () {\small $G\mapsto Z_G(\lambda)$};  
        \draw[->,-latex] (C2)--(Z) node[midway,below,sloped] () {\small $\Sigma$};  
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
    Here, $\Sigma: \mathbb{C}^{2^k}\to \mathbb{C}$ denotes the projection map given by the sum of the coordinates, and $\mathbb{C}^{2^k}\dashrightarrow \mathbb{P}^{2^{k}-1}$ represents the canonical projection map.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
Since we will consistently consider the dynamics on $\mathbb{P}^{2^k-1}$ rather than on $\mathbb{C}^{2^k}$, we have chosen to use the simpler notation $F_\lambda$ (without the hat) for the map on projective space, and similarly use the hatless notation $\varphi_\lambda$ for the map from $\mathcal{G}_k$ into projective space. In our previous paper \cite{HP2024}, the roles of $F_\lambda$ and $\widehat{F}_\lambda$ were reversed.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- convention-1: body_md -->
Given a graph $G\in \mathcal{G}_k$, a binary $k$-tuple ${\bf x}=(x_1,\dots, x_k)\in \{0,1\}^k$, and  a fixed parameter $\lambda\in \mathbb{C}$, we will use the shorter notation $({\bf x})=(\!(x_1,\dots, x_k)\!)$ for $Z_G^{\bf x}(\lambda)$ and $({\bf x})^\prime=(\!(x_1,\dots, x_k)\!)'$ for $Z_{\mathcal{R}(G)}^{\bf x}(\lambda)$, so that the respective self-map $F_\lambda$ on the projective space $\mathbb{P}^{2^k-1}$ is given by
$$
[\, (\!(0,\ldots, 0)\!): \cdots : (\!(1,\ldots, 1)\!) \,] \mapsto [\, (\!(0,\ldots, 0)\!)^\prime: \cdots : (\!(1,\ldots, 1)\!)^\prime \,].
$$
In fact, we will also use the notation $({\bf x})=(\!(x_1,\dots, x_k)\!)$ for the respective coordinate in $\mathbb{C}^{2^k}$. Furthermore, we write ${\bf 0} := (0, \dots , 0)$ for
the zero vector and ${\bf e}_j := (0, \dots, 0, 1, 0, \dots, 0)$ for the $j$-th unit vector in $\{0,1\}^k$ for each $j\in \{1,\dots,k\}$.
<!-- end convention-1 -->
<!-- example-7: body_md -->
Let us revisit the recursion operator $\mathcal{R}$ associated with the Sierpi\'nski gluing data $(H, \Phi)$ with parameters $k=m=3$ from Example~\ref{ex: sierpinksi}. Recall that $V(H)=\{1,2,3\}$ and the graph $H$ has six hyperedges: three $1$-edges $\{1\}, \{2\}, \{3\}$ and three $2$-edges $\{1,2\}, \{1,3\}, \{2,3\}$. Furthermore, we have  $\Phi(j)=\{j\}$ for each $j\in \{1,2,3\}$. Fix $G\in \mathcal{G}_k$ and let ${\bf x} = (x_1, x_2, x_3) \in \{0,1\}^3$ be a vertex assignment on the labeled vertices of $\mathcal{R}(G)$, which correspond to the three $1$-edges in $E(H)$. As before, let $Y$ denote the set of vertices of $\mathcal{R}(G)$ that correspond to the edges in $E(H)\setminus\Phi(\{1,2,3\})$, that is, to the $2$-edges in $E(H)$. Consider all possible vertex assignments $\eta$ on $Y$, encoded by binary triples ${\bf y}=(y_1, y_2, y_3)$, so that $y_j$ is the value received by the vertex of $\mathcal{R}(G)$ corresponding to the $2$-edge $\{1,2,3\}\setminus\{j\}$ for each $j\in \{1,2,3\}$. Following the convention above, we obtain from \eqref{eq: x_prime_conditioned} that the  corresponding homogeneous self-map $\widehat{F}_\lambda: \mathbb{C}^8\to\mathbb{C}^8$ is given by
    $$
    (\!(x_1, x_2, x_3)\!)^\prime = \sum_{{\bf y}\in \{0,1\}^3} \lambda^{-(y_1+y_2+y_3)} \cdot (\!(x_1, y_2, y_3)\!) (\!(y_1, x_2, y_3)\!) (\!(y_1, y_2, x_3)\!).
    $$
<!-- end example-7 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
We say that there is \emph{no direct correlation} between the marked vertices of a graph $G\in \mathcal{G}_k$ at a given parameter $\lambda\in \mathbb{C}$ if for each pair of vertex assignments $\tau$ and $\sigma$ on disjoint subsets of the marked vertices of $G$ we have
    \begin{equation}\label{eq: no direct correlation}
        Z_G(\lambda) \cdot Z_G^{\tau\wedge\sigma}(\lambda) -Z_G^\tau(\lambda) \cdot  Z_G^\sigma(\lambda) = 0.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
We say that there is \emph{no correlation} between the marked vertices of a graph $G\in \mathcal{G}_k$ at a given parameter $\lambda\in \mathbb{C}$ if for each triple of vertex assignments $\tau$, $\sigma$, and $\eta$ on pairwise disjoint subsets of the marked vertices of $G$ we have
    \begin{equation}\label{eq: no correlation}
        Z_G^{\tau\wedge \sigma \wedge \eta}(\lambda) \cdot Z_G^{\eta}(\lambda) -Z_G^{\tau \wedge\eta}(\lambda) \cdot  Z_G^{\sigma \wedge \eta}(\lambda) = 0.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem: direct vs conditional correlation}
    When $\lambda\in \mathbb{C}$ satisfies $Z_G(\lambda) \neq 0$, absence of direct correlations between the marked vertices of $G$ at $\lambda$ implies absence of correlations between the marked vertices of $G$ at $\lambda$.
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Suppose $\tau$, $\sigma$, and $\eta$ are arbitrary vertex assignments on pairwise disjoint subsets of the marked vertices of $G$. Using~\eqref{eq: no direct correlation}, we deduce
    \[
    Z_G(\lambda)\cdot Z_G^{\tau\wedge \sigma \wedge \eta}(\lambda)\cdot Z_G^{\eta}(\lambda) = Z_G^{\tau\wedge \eta}(\lambda) \cdot  Z_G^{\sigma}(\lambda)\cdot Z_G^{\eta}(\lambda)
    = Z_G^{\tau\wedge \eta}(\lambda) \cdot Z_G(\lambda)\cdot Z_G^{\sigma \wedge \eta} (\lambda).
    \]
    Therefore, \eqref{eq: no correlation} holds whenever $Z_G(\lambda) \neq 0$.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lem: inv variety}
    There is no correlation between the marked vertices of $G\in\mathcal{G}_k$ at $\lambda\in \mathbb{C}$ if and only if the coordinates  $({\bf x}) = Z^{{\bf x}}_G(\lambda)$, ${\bf x}\in\{0,1\}^k$, of $\widehat{\varphi}_\lambda(G)$ satisfy the homogeneous quadratic equations
    \begin{equation}\label{eq: no correlation variety}
    ({\bf x}) \cdot ({\bf y}) = ({\bf z}) \cdot({\bf w}),
    \end{equation}
    where ${\bf x},\, {\bf y}, {\bf z}, {\bf w}\in \{0,1\}^{k}$ run over all possible binary $k$-tuples satisfying ${\bf x}+{\bf y}={\bf z}+{\bf w}$.
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Let us first show that if equations~\eqref{eq: no correlation variety} are satisfied by the coordinates of $\widehat{\varphi}_\lambda(G)$ for some graph $G\in \mathcal{G}_k$ and $\lambda\in \mathbb{C}$, then there is no correlation between the marked vertices of $G$ at $\lambda$. Suppose we are given three vertex assignments $\tau$, $\sigma$, and $\eta$ on pairwise disjoint subsets $S_\tau$, $S_\sigma$, and $S_\eta$ of the marked set $P=P(G)$, respectively. Let $S_\upsilon:=P\setminus (S_\tau\sqcup S_\sigma\sqcup S_\eta)$ and $\upsilon$ be an arbitrary vertex assignment on $S_\upsilon$. Then $\tau\wedge \sigma\wedge \eta\wedge\upsilon$ is an assignment on the full marked set $P$, and thus it may be naturally viewed as a binary $k$-tuple representing a coordinate in $\mathbb{C}^{2^k}$.  Using~\eqref{eq: no correlation variety}, we then obtain  
\begin{align*}
Z_G^{\tau\wedge \sigma \wedge \eta}(\lambda) \cdot Z_G^{\eta}(\lambda) &= \sum_{\upsilon: S_\upsilon\to\{0,1\} }Z_G^{\tau\wedge \sigma \wedge \eta\wedge \upsilon}(\lambda) \;\; \times \sum_{\substack{{\widetilde{\tau}: S_\tau\to\{0,1\},\;\widetilde{\sigma}: S_\sigma\to\{0,1\},} \\ {\widetilde{\upsilon}: S_\upsilon\to\{0,1\}}}} Z_G^{\widetilde{\tau}\wedge \widetilde{\sigma} \wedge \eta\wedge \widetilde{\upsilon}}(\lambda)\\
&= \sum_{\substack{{\widetilde{\tau}: S_\tau\to\{0,1\},\;\widetilde{\sigma}: S_\sigma\to\{0,1\},} \\ {\widetilde{\upsilon}: S_\upsilon\to\{0,1\}}, \; \upsilon: S_\upsilon\to\{0,1\}}} (\tau\wedge \sigma \wedge \eta\wedge \upsilon)\cdot (\widetilde{\tau}\wedge \widetilde{\sigma} \wedge \eta\wedge \widetilde{\upsilon})\\
&= \sum_{\substack{{\widetilde{\tau}: S_\tau\to\{0,1\},\;\widetilde{\sigma}: S_\sigma\to\{0,1\},} \\ {\widetilde{\upsilon}: S_\upsilon\to\{0,1\}}, \; \upsilon: S_\upsilon\to\{0,1\}}} (\tau\wedge \widetilde{\sigma} \wedge \eta\wedge \upsilon)\cdot (\widetilde{\tau}\wedge \sigma \wedge \eta\wedge \widetilde{\upsilon})\\
&= \sum_{\substack{\widetilde{\sigma}: S_\sigma\to\{0,1\},\\ \upsilon: S_\upsilon\to\{0,1\}}} Z_G^{\tau\wedge \widetilde{\sigma} \wedge \eta\wedge \upsilon}(\lambda)\;\; \times \sum_{\substack{\widetilde{\tau}: S_\tau\to\{0,1\},\\ \widetilde{\upsilon}: S_\upsilon\to\{0,1\}}} Z_G^{\widetilde{\tau}\wedge \sigma \wedge \eta\wedge \widetilde{\upsilon}}(\lambda)\\
&= Z_G^{\tau\wedge \eta}(\lambda) \cdot Z_G^{\sigma\wedge\eta}(\lambda),
\end{align*}
which implies the desired identity~\eqref{eq: no correlation}.

To prove the converse, assume that there is no correlation between the marked vertices of $G$ at some $\lambda\in \mathbb{C}$.
Let ${\bf x},{\bf y} \in \{0,1\}^k$ be two arbitrary binary $k$-tuples. By~\eqref{eq: inv reduction}, it is sufficient to prove that
$$
({\bf x}) \cdot  ({\bf y}) = (\max({\bf x},{\bf y})) \cdot (\min({\bf x},{\bf y})),
$$
where $({\bf x}) = Z^{{\bf x}}_G(\lambda)$ and $({\bf y}) = Z^{{\bf y}}_G(\lambda)$. 

Let $S\subset P$ be the subset of marked vertices receiving identical values from ${\bf x}$ and ${\bf y}$. On the remaining marked vertices, exactly one of ${\bf x}$ and ${\bf y}$ assigns a $1$ and the other assigns a $0$. Let $Q$ (resp.\ $R$) be the subset of those marked vertices that receive a $1$ (resp.\ a $0$) from ${\bf x}$ and a $0$ (resp.\ a $1$) from~${\bf y}$. Clearly, the sets $Q$, $R$, and $S$ form a partition of $P$. 

Let $\upsilon$ denote the vertex assignment on $S$ that assigns the same value to each vertex as ${\bf x}$ (and ${\bf y}$). Following the notation in Section~\ref{subsec: indep-poly}, the vertex assignments ${\bf x}$ and ${\bf y}$ coincide with ${\bf 1}_Q \wedge {\bf 0}_R \wedge \upsilon$ and ${\bf 0}_Q \wedge {\bf 1}_R \wedge \upsilon$ , respectively. Consequently, our goal is to show that 
$$
({\bf 1}_Q \wedge {\bf 0}_R \wedge \upsilon) \cdot  ({\bf 0}_Q \wedge {\bf 1}_R \wedge \upsilon) = ({\bf 1}_Q \wedge {\bf 1}_R \wedge \upsilon) \cdot ({\bf 0}_Q \wedge {\bf 0}_R \wedge \upsilon).
$$


To simplify notation, given any partial vertex assignment $\zeta$ on $P$ we will write $(\zeta)$ for $Z^{\zeta}_G(\lambda)$ below. Using the inclusion--exclusion principle twice, we can then write 
\begin{align*}
({\bf 0}_Q\wedge 
{\bf 1}_R \wedge\upsilon) &= \sum_{{\widetilde Q}\subset Q} (-1)^{\#{\widetilde Q}} \cdot  ({\bf 1}_{\widetilde Q} \wedge {\bf 1}_R\wedge  \upsilon)\\ &=\sum_{{\widetilde Q}\subset Q} (-1)^{\#{\widetilde Q}} \sum_{{\widetilde R}\subset R} (-1)^{\#{\widetilde R}}\cdot ({\bf 1}_{\widetilde Q} \wedge {\bf 0}_{\widetilde R}\wedge  \upsilon)\\
&=\sum_{{\widetilde Q}\subset Q,\, {\widetilde R}\subset R} (-1)^{\#{\widetilde Q}+\#{\widetilde R}} \cdot ({\bf 1}_{\widetilde Q} \wedge {\bf 0}_{\widetilde R}\wedge  \upsilon)
\end{align*}
and thus
\[
({\bf 1}_Q \wedge {\bf 0}_R \wedge \upsilon) \cdot  ({\bf 0}_Q\wedge {\bf 1}_R\wedge \upsilon) = \sum_{{\widetilde Q}\subset Q,\, {\widetilde R}\subset R} (-1)^{\#{\widetilde Q}+\#{\widetilde R}} \cdot ({\bf 1}_Q \wedge {\bf 0}_R \wedge \upsilon) \cdot ({\bf 1}_{\widetilde Q} \wedge {\bf 0}_{\widetilde R}\wedge  \upsilon).
\]



By the assumption of no correlation between the marked vertices of $G$ at $\lambda$, for all subsets ${\widetilde Q}\subset Q$ and ${\widetilde R}\subset R$ we have
\[
({\bf 1}_Q \wedge {\bf 0}_R \wedge \upsilon) \cdot  ({\bf 1}_{\widetilde Q} \wedge {\bf 0}_{\widetilde R} \wedge \upsilon) = ({\bf 1}_Q \wedge {\bf 0}_{\widetilde R} \wedge \upsilon) \cdot  ({\bf 1}_{\widetilde Q} \wedge {\bf 0}_R \wedge \upsilon),
\]
where we have applied \eqref{eq: no correlation} with $\tau = {\bf 1}_{Q\setminus {\widetilde Q}}$, $\sigma={\bf 0}_{R\setminus {\widetilde R}}$, and $\eta={\bf 1}_{\widetilde Q}\wedge {\bf 0}_{\widetilde R}\wedge\upsilon$. It follows that 
\begin{align*}
({\bf 1}_Q \wedge {\bf 0}_R \wedge \upsilon) \cdot  ({\bf 0}_Q\wedge {\bf 1}_R\wedge \upsilon) &= \sum_{{\widetilde Q}\subset Q,\, {\widetilde R}\subset R} (-1)^{\#{\widetilde Q}+\#{\widetilde R}} \cdot ({\bf 1}_Q \wedge {\bf 0}_{\widetilde R} \wedge \upsilon) \cdot  ({\bf 1}_{\widetilde Q} \wedge {\bf 0}_R \wedge \upsilon)\\
&= \left(\sum_{{\widetilde R}\subset R}(-1)^{\#{\widetilde R}} \cdot ({\bf 1}_Q \wedge {\bf 0}_{\widetilde R} \wedge \upsilon)\right)\times \left(\sum_{{\widetilde Q}\subset Q}(-1)^{\#{\widetilde Q}} \cdot ({\bf 1}_{\widetilde{Q}}\wedge {\bf 0}_{R} \wedge \upsilon)\right)\\
&=({\bf 1}_Q \wedge {\bf 1}_R \wedge \upsilon) \cdot ({\bf 0}_Q \wedge {\bf 0}_R \wedge \upsilon),
\end{align*}
where we have used the inclusion--exclusion principle twice to derive the last equality. Consequently, \[({\bf x}) \cdot  ({\bf y}) = (\max({\bf x},{\bf y})) \cdot (\min({\bf x},{\bf y})).\]
Since ${\bf x}$ and ${\bf y}$ were arbitrary binary $k$-tuples, we conclude that equations~\eqref{eq: no correlation variety} hold, which finishes the proof.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- definition-7: body_md -->
\label{def: inv variety}
    Let $k\in \mathbb N$ and $\mathcal{M} \subset \mathbb{P}^{2^k-1}$ be the algebraic subvariety defined by the homogeneous quadratic equations 
    \begin{equation*}
    ({\bf x}) \cdot ({\bf y}) = ({\bf z}) \cdot ({\bf w}),
    \end{equation*}
    where ${\bf x},\, {\bf y}, {\bf z}, {\bf w}\in \{0,1\}^{k}$ run over all possible binary $k$-tuples satisfying ${\bf x}+{\bf y}={\bf z}+{\bf w}$. We call $\mathcal{M}$ the \emph{subvariety of uncorrelated marked vertices} or simply the \emph{invariant variety} for $\mathcal{G}_k$.
<!-- end definition-7 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lem: Segre}
    For each $k\in \mathbb N$ the invariant variety $\mathcal{M} \subset \mathbb{P}^{2^k-1}$ for $\mathcal{G}_k$ coincides with the $k$-fold Segre variety, which is a smooth and irreducible $k$-dimensional subvariety of $\mathbb{P}^{2^k-1}$.
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- prop-1: body_md -->
\label{prop: invariance}
    The subvariety $\mathcal{M}$ of uncorrelated marked vertices for $\mathcal{G}_k$ (see Definition~\ref{def: inv variety}) is invariant under the renormalization map $F_\lambda$ (outside its indeterminacy set).
<!-- end prop-1 -->
<!-- corollary-4: body_md -->
\label{cor: invariance for graphs}
    Let $\lambda\in \mathbb R_+$ and $G\in \mathcal{G}_k$. Assume that there is no correlation between the marked vertices of $G$ at $\lambda$. Then there is no correlation between the marked vertices of $\mathcal{R}(G)$ at $\lambda$.
<!-- end corollary-4 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Since $\lambda\in \mathbb R_+$, we have $Z_G(\lambda) \neq 0$, and hence by Lemma~\ref{lem: direct vs conditional correlation} it is sufficient to show absence of direct correlations between the marked vertices of $\mathcal{R}(G)$ at $\lambda$. To simplify the notation, we set $\mathbb{P}:=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{R}(G),\lambda}$. 

    Suppose $\tau$ and $\sigma$ are arbitrary vertex assignments on two disjoint subsets  $X$ and $\widetilde{X}$ of the marked vertices of $\mathcal{R}(G)$. We need to check that 
    \[
    \mathbb{P}[\tau \wedge \sigma]= \mathbb{P}[\tau] \cdot\mathbb{P}[\sigma].
    \]
    Since this clearly holds when $\mathbb{P}[\sigma]=0$, we will assume in the following that $\mathbb{P}[\sigma]\neq 0$. Then, it suffices to check that
    \[
    \mathbb{P}[\tau: \sigma]= \mathbb{P}[\tau].
    \]
    
    As in Section~\ref{subsec: induced dynamics}, let $Y\subset V(\mathcal{R}(G))$ denote the set of vertices of $\mathcal{R}(G)$ that are induced by the edges in $E(H)\setminus \Phi(\{1,\dots,k\})$.   
     Since there is no correlation between the marked vertices of $G$, the recursive construction of $\mathcal{R}(G)$ implies that there is a constant $C(\tau)$ such that
     \[
         \mathbb{P}[\tau: \eta \wedge \sigma] = C(\tau) \tag{\textasteriskcentered} \label{eq: main prob fact}
     \]
    for all assignments $\eta: Y \rightarrow \{0,1\}$ with $\mathbb{P}[\eta\wedge \sigma]\neq 0$.
    
    We may now write: 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq: direct conditioned}
    \begin{aligned}
    \mathbb{P} [\tau : \sigma] &= \sum_{\eta: Y \rightarrow \{0,1\}} \mathbb{P} [\tau \wedge \eta : \sigma] = \sum_{\substack{\eta: Y \rightarrow \{0,1\},\\ \mathbb{P}[\eta\wedge \sigma]\neq 0}} \mathbb{P} [\tau \wedge \eta : \sigma] \\
    &=  \sum_{\substack{\eta: Y \rightarrow \{0,1\},\\ \mathbb{P}[\eta\wedge \sigma]\neq 0}} \mathbb{P} [\tau : \eta \wedge \sigma] \cdot \mathbb{P} [\eta : \sigma] =  \sum_{\substack{\eta: Y \rightarrow \{0,1\},\\ \mathbb{P}[\eta\wedge \sigma]\neq 0}} C(\tau) \cdot \mathbb{P} [\eta : \sigma] \\
    &=   C(\tau) \cdot \sum_{\substack{\eta: Y \rightarrow \{0,1\},\\ \mathbb{P}[\eta\wedge \sigma]\neq 0}} \mathbb{P} [\eta : \sigma] =   C(\tau).
    \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}

    Since \eqref{eq: direct conditioned} holds for all assignments $\sigma$ on $\widetilde{X}$ with $\mathbb{P}[\sigma]\neq0$, it follows that 
    \[
   \mathbb{P}[\tau]= \sum_{\substack{\sigma: \widetilde{X} \to \{0,1\},\\ \mathbb{P}[\sigma]\neq 0}} \mathbb{P}[\tau: \sigma] \cdot\mathbb{P}[\sigma] = C(\tau ) \cdot \sum_{\substack{\sigma: \widetilde{X} \to \{0,1\},\\ \mathbb{P}[\sigma]\neq 0}} \mathbb{P}[\sigma] = C(\tau),
    \]
   which finishes the proof.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
Suppose the coordinates $({\bf x})$, ${\bf x}\in \{0,1\}^k$, of a point $\widehat{\xi}\in \mathbb{C}^{2^k}$ satisfy equations~\eqref{eq: no correlation variety}. We will show that the coordinates $({\bf x})'$, ${\bf x}\in \{0,1\}^k$, of the image point $\widehat{\xi}':=\widehat{F}_\lambda(\widehat \xi )\in \mathbb{C}^{2^k}$ satisfy these equations as well. The proposition then immediately follows. 

    Although the coordinates of $\widehat{\xi}$ may not correspond to the values of conditioned independence polynomials for an actual graph $G\in \mathcal{G}_k$, we temporarily regard them as such in order to recall the definition of the image coordinates $({\bf x})^\prime$, ${\bf x}\in\{0,1\}^k$. We then view ${\bf x}$ as a vertex assignment on the set $P(\mathcal{R}(G))$ of the marked vertices of the graph $\mathcal{R}(G)$. By the discussion in Section~\ref{subsec: induced dynamics}, we have
    \[
      ({\bf x})' =\sum_{\eta: Y\to \{0,1\}} \lambda^{-\|{\bf x}\wedge \eta\|_H} \prod_{i=1}^m ([{\bf x}\wedge \eta]_i),
    \]
    where    
    \[
    \|{\bf x} \wedge \eta\|_H  =\sum_{i=1}^m
    \#[{\bf x}\wedge\eta]_i^{-1}(1)- \#({\bf x}\wedge \eta)^{-1}(1).
    \]    
    Here, $Y$ is the set of vertices of $\mathcal{R}(G)$ that are induced by the edges in $E(H) \setminus \Phi(\{1,\dots, k\})$, and each $[{\bf x}\wedge \eta]_i\in \{0,1\}^k$, $i\in\{1,\dots,m\}$, denotes the assignment on the marked vertices of the $i$-th copy $G(i)$ induced by the vertex assignment ${\bf x}\wedge \eta$ on $P(\mathcal{R}(G))\sqcup Y$. 
       

    To check that the coordinates of $\widehat{\xi}'$ satisfy  equations~\eqref{eq: no correlation variety}, it suffices to verify that
    \[
        ({\bf x})' \cdot (\widetilde{{\bf x}})'=(\max({\bf x}, \widetilde{{\bf x}}))' \cdot  (\min({\bf x}, \widetilde{{\bf x}}))'
    \]
    for all ${\bf x}, \widetilde{{\bf x}}\in \{0,1\}^k$. 
    Note that for all assignments $\eta, \widetilde{\eta}$ on $Y$  we have 
    \[{\bf x}\wedge \eta + \widetilde{{\bf x}}\wedge \widetilde{\eta}=\max({\bf x}, \widetilde{{\bf x}}) \wedge \eta + \min({\bf x}, \widetilde{{\bf x}})\wedge \widetilde{\eta},\]
    as well as 
    \[[{\bf x}\wedge \eta]_i + [\widetilde{{\bf x}}\wedge \widetilde{\eta}]_i=[\max({\bf x}, \widetilde{{\bf x}}) \wedge \eta]_i + [\min({\bf x}, \widetilde{{\bf x}})\wedge \widetilde{\eta}]_i\]
    for each $i\in \{1,\dots, m\}$, where we view each summand as a binary vector representing the corresponding vertex assignment. In particular, we have
    \[
    \|{\bf x}\wedge \eta\|_H + \|\widetilde{{\bf x}}\wedge \widetilde{\eta}\|_H = \|\max({\bf x}, \widetilde{{\bf x}}) \wedge \eta\|_H + \|\min({\bf x}, \widetilde{{\bf x}})\wedge \widetilde{\eta}\|_H,
    \]
    as well as 
    \[([{\bf x}\wedge \eta]_i) \cdot ([\widetilde{{\bf x}}\wedge \widetilde{\eta}]_i)= ([\max({\bf x}, \widetilde{{\bf x}}) \wedge \eta]_i) \cdot ([\min({\bf x}, \widetilde{{\bf x}})\wedge \widetilde{\eta}]_i)\]
    for each $i\in \{1,\dots, m\}$. 


    Using the relations above, we compute:
     \begin{align*}
        ({\bf x})'\cdot (\widetilde{{\bf x}})'&= \left(\sum_{\eta: Y\to \{0,1\}} \lambda^{-\|{\bf x}\wedge \eta\|_H} \prod_{i=1}^m ([{\bf x}\wedge \eta]_i)\right) \cdot
        \left(\sum_{\widetilde{\eta}: Y\to \{0,1\}} \lambda^{-\|\widetilde{{\bf x}}\wedge \widetilde{\eta}\|_H} \prod_{i=1}^m ([\widetilde{{\bf x}}\wedge \widetilde{\eta}]_i)\right)\\
        &= \sum_{\eta, \, \widetilde{\eta}: Y\to \{0,1\}} \lambda^{-\|{\bf x}\wedge \eta\|_H}\lambda^{-\|\widetilde{{\bf x}}\wedge \widetilde{\eta}\|_H} \prod_{i=1}^m ([{\bf x}\wedge \eta]_i)([\widetilde{{\bf x}}\wedge \widetilde{\eta}]_i)\\
         &= \sum_{\eta, \, \widetilde{\eta}: Y\to \{0,1\}} \lambda^{-\|\max({\bf x}, \widetilde{{\bf x}}) \wedge \eta\|_H}\lambda^{-\|\min({\bf x}, \widetilde{{\bf x}}) \wedge \widetilde{\eta}\|_H} \prod_{i=1}^m ([\max({\bf x}, \widetilde{{\bf x}}) \wedge \eta]_i) ([\min({\bf x}, \widetilde{{\bf x}})\wedge \widetilde{\eta}]_i)\\
         &=(\max({\bf x}, \widetilde{{\bf x}}))'\cdot  (\min({\bf x}, \widetilde{{\bf x}}))'.
    \end{align*}
    This finishes the proof.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lem: graph on 0-chart}
    On the chart $\{({\bf 0}) \neq 0\}$, the action of $F_\lambda$ on the invariant variety $\mathcal{M}$ is governed by the equations
    \[
    ({\bf x})'= ({\bf 0})'\cdot \prod_{j:\; x_j=1} \lambda^{1-\#\Phi(j)}  \left(\frac{({\bf e}_{\Lambda(j)})}{({\bf 0})}\right)^{\#\Phi(j)}
    \]
    for all ${\bf x}=(x_1,\dots, x_k)\in \{0,1\}^k\setminus \{{\bf 0}\}$.
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Fix an arbitrary ${\bf x}=(x_1,\dots, x_k)\in \{0,1\}^k\setminus \{{\bf 0}\}$. Following the notation in the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop: invariance}, for every vertex assignment $\eta$ on the set $Y$, we have
\begin{align*}
    \|{\bf x} \wedge \eta\|_H  &=\sum_{i=1}^m
    \#[{\bf x}\wedge\eta]_i^{-1}(1)- \#({\bf x}\wedge \eta)^{-1}(1)\\
    &=\sum_{i=1}^m
    \#[{\bf 0}\wedge\eta]_i^{-1}(1)- \#({\bf 0}\wedge \eta)^{-1}(1) + \sum_{j:\; x_j=1}(\#\Phi(j) - 1)\\
    &= \|{\bf 0}\wedge \eta\|_H +  \sum_{j:\; x_j=1}(\#\Phi(j) - 1).
\end{align*}
At the same time, using~\eqref{eq: variety in basis coordinates}, for each $i\in \{1,\dots, m\}$ we have
\[ 
([{\bf x}\wedge \eta]_i) = ([{\bf 0}\wedge \eta]_i) \cdot \prod_{j:\; i\in \Phi(j) \text{ and } x_j=1} \frac{({\bf e}_{\Lambda(j)})}{({\bf 0})}, 
\]
and thus 
\[
    \prod_{i=1}^m ([{\bf x}\wedge \eta]_i) = \prod_{i=1}^m ([{\bf 0}\wedge \eta]_i) \cdot  \prod_{j:\; x_j=1} \left(\frac{({\bf e}_{\Lambda(j)})}{({\bf 0})}\right)^{\#\Phi(j)}.
\]
It follows that 
\begin{align*}
      ({\bf x})' &=\sum_{\eta: Y\to \{0,1\}} \lambda^{-\|{\bf x}\wedge \eta\|_H} \prod_{i=1}^m ([{\bf x}\wedge \eta]_i) \\
      &=  \prod_{j:\; x_j=1} \lambda^{1-\#\Phi(j)}\left(\frac{({\bf e}_{\Lambda(j)})}{({\bf 0})}\right)^{\#\Phi(j)}\cdot \sum_{\eta: Y\to \{0,1\}} \lambda^{-\|{\bf 0}\wedge \eta\|_H} \prod_{i=1}^m ([{\bf 0}\wedge \eta]_i) \\
      &= ({\bf 0})'\cdot \prod_{j:\; x_j=1} \lambda^{1-\#\Phi(j)} \left(\frac{({\bf e}_{\Lambda(j)})}{({\bf 0})}\right)^{\#\Phi(j)},
\end{align*}
which completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- definition-8: body_md -->
A gluing data $(H,\Phi)$ with parameters $k,m$ (and associated graph recursion $\mathcal{R} = \mathcal{R}_{(H,\Phi)}$) is called \emph{pre-fixed} if the induced dynamical system $\Lambda=\Lambda_{(H,\Phi)}$ is pre-fixed, that is, $\Lambda^2=\Lambda$ on $\{1,\dots, k\}$.
<!-- end definition-8 -->
<!-- prop-2: body_md -->
\label{prop: pre fixed dynamics}
    Suppose the gluing data $(H, \Phi)$ is pre-fixed, and let $1 \leq k_0\leq k$ be the number of periodic labels, that is, $k_0=\#\Lambda(\{1,\dots, k\})$. Then, for each $\lambda\in \mathbb{C}^*$, $F_\lambda$ maps (the regular part of) the invariant variety $\mathcal{M}$ to an irreducible $k_0$-dimensional subvariety $\mathcal{M}_0= \mathcal{M}_0(\lambda) \subset \mathcal{M}$, which depends continuously on $\lambda\in \mathbb{C}^*$ in the Hausdorff metric. The regular part $\mathcal{M}_0\setminus\mathrm{I}(F_\lambda)$ of $\mathcal{M}_0$ is invariant under the renormalization map $F_\lambda$.


    Moreover, if $(H, \Phi)$ is also non-degenerate, then $\mathcal{M}_0$ is smooth and $F_\lambda$ restricts to the identity on the regular part of $\mathcal{M}_0$:
      \begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[->,>={Stealth[round]},shorten >=1pt,auto,semithick]
  \node (M) {$\mathcal{M}$};
  \node (M0) [right=2cm of M] {$\mathcal{M}_0$};
  \draw[->, dashed] (M) to node {$F_\lambda$} (M0);
  \draw[->, dashed] (M0) edge[loop right] node[right] {\small $F_\lambda=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{M}_0}$} ();
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
Furthermore, the subvarieties $\mathcal{M}_0(\lambda)$ form a holomorphic family in $\lambda$: \[\mathcal{Y}:=\{(\lambda, \xi): \lambda\in \mathbb{C}^*, \xi\in \mathcal{M}_0(\lambda)\}\] is a complex submanifold of $\mathbb{C}^*\times \mathbb{P}^{2^k-1}$.
<!-- end prop-2 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Up to renaming, we may assume that the labels $1,\dots, k_0$ are periodic. Since $(H,\Phi)$ is pre-fixed, we then have $\Lambda(j)=j$ for each label $j\leq k_0$, and $\Lambda(j)\in \{1,\dots, k_0\}$ for all $j>k_0$. 
    

    
    Consider the monomial map $\Psi_{0, \lambda}: (\mathbb{C}^*)^{k_0}\to \mathbb{P}^{2^k-1}$ defined by  
    \[(e_1,\dots, e_{k_0}) \mapsto [1: \cdots:\prod_{j:\; x_j=1} \lambda^{1-\#\Phi(j)}  \,(e_{\Lambda(j)})^{\#\Phi(j)}: \cdots ],\]
    meaning that the $(\!(x_1, \ldots, x_k)\!)$-coordinate of the image is given by the monomial 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq: x-coordinate of Psi_0}
    \prod_{j:\; x_j=1} \lambda^{1-\#\Phi(j)} \, (e_{\Lambda(j)})^{\#\Phi(j)},
    \end{equation}
    except for the $({\bf 0})$-coordinate, which equals $1$. In particular, for each periodic label $j=1,\dots, k_0$, the $({\bf e}_j)$-coordinate of the image equals \[\lambda^{1-\#\Phi(j)} \, (e_{j})^{\#\Phi(j)}.\] 
    
    
    Let $Y_{\lambda}$ be the image of $\Psi_{0, \lambda}$ and $\mathcal{M}_0=\mathcal{M}_0(\lambda)$ be its Zariski closure. Since $\Psi_{0,\lambda}$ is a regular map with finite fibers for each $\lambda\in \mathbb{C}^*$, irreducibility and dimension are preserved, that is, $\mathcal{M}_0$ is an irreducible $k_0$-dimensional variety in $\mathbb{P}^{2^k-1}$. By \eqref{eq: variety in basis coordinates}, the set $Y_{\lambda}$, and thus its Zariski closure $\mathcal{M}_0$, is contained in the invariant variety $\mathcal{M}$. Moreover, by Lemma~\ref{lem: graph on 0-chart}, $F_\lambda$ maps the regular part of $\mathcal{M}\cap \{({\bf 0}) \neq 0\}$ into $Y_{\lambda}\subset \mathcal{M}_0$. Since $\left(\mathcal{M}\cap \{({\bf 0}) \neq 0\}\right) \setminus \mathrm{I}(F_\lambda)$ is open and dense in $\mathcal{M}$, it follows that $\mathcal{M}_0$ contains the image $F_\lambda(\mathcal{M} \setminus \mathrm{I}(F_\lambda))$. In particular, the regular part $\mathcal{M}_0 \setminus \mathrm{I}(F_\lambda)$ is invariant under $F_\lambda$. Finally, since $\Psi_{0,\lambda}$ is given by the monomials \eqref{eq: x-coordinate of Psi_0}, the subvariety $\mathcal{M}_0=\mathcal{M}_0(\lambda)$ is the closure of the image $Y_\lambda=\Psi_{0,\lambda}((\mathbb{C}^*)^{k_0})$ also in the Euclidean topology induced from $\mathbb{C}^{2^k}$, and thus varies continuously with $\lambda$. 
    
    Now suppose that $(H, \Phi)$ is non-degenerate. Then for each periodic label $j=1,\dots, k_0$, we have $\#\Phi(j)=1$, and thus the $({\bf e}_j)$-coordinate of $\Psi_{0,\lambda}\big((e_1,\dots, e_{k_0})\big)$ equals $e_j$. (In particular, $\Psi_{0,\lambda}$ is an embedding in this case.) By Lemma~\ref{lem: graph on 0-chart}, $F_\lambda$ fixes each point in the open dense subset $Y_{\lambda}\setminus \mathrm{I}(F_\lambda)$ of  $\mathcal{M}_0$. We conclude that $F_\lambda$ fixes all points of $\mathcal{M}_0\setminus \mathrm{I}(F_\lambda)$, i.e., it restricts to the identity on the regular part of $\mathcal{M}_0$. It remains to show that $\mathcal{M}_0$ is smooth. 
    
    The definition of $\Psi_{0,\lambda}$ implies that it is sufficient to prove smoothness of $\mathcal{M}_0=\mathcal{M}_0(\lambda)$ for $\lambda = 1$. In this case, $\mathcal{M}_0$ is the projective toric variety associated with the finite set $\mathscr{A}_0\subset \mathbb Z^{k_0}$, consisting of the exponent vectors of the coordinate monomials of $\Psi_{0,1}$, and realized in $\mathbb{P}^{2^k-1}$ via the monomial embedding $\Psi_{0,1}$. More explicitly, for each coordinate $({\bf x})$, ${\bf x}=(x_1,\dots, x_k)\in \{0,1\}^k$, the $i$-th entry of the corresponding exponent vector $v_{({\bf x})}\in \mathscr{A}_0\subset \mathbb Z^{k_0}$ is given by 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq: exponents}
    \sum_{j:\; \Lambda(j)=i \text{ and } x_j=1} \#\Phi(j),
    \end{equation}
    where the sum is understood to be zero if empty. It is straightforward to verify that the convex hull $\mathscr{P}_0:=\operatorname{conv}(\mathscr{A}_0)$ is the $k_0$-dimensional hyperbox in $\mathbb R^{k_0}$ with opposite vertices \[v_{({\bf 0})}=(0,0, \dots, 0) \text{\; and  \;} 
    v_{(\!(1,\dots,1)\!)}=\left(\sum_{j:\; \Lambda(j)=1} \#\Phi(j), \sum_{j:\; \Lambda(j)=2} \#\Phi(j),  \dots, \sum_{j:\; \Lambda(j)=k_0} \#\Phi(j)\right).\]
    Note that, since $\mathscr{P}_0$ may contain $\mathbb Z^{k_0}$-lattice points that are not in $\mathscr{A}_0$,  the variety $\mathcal{M}_0$ is not necessarily the projective toric variety associated with the polytope $\mathscr{P}_0=\operatorname{conv}(\mathscr{A}_0)$ itself. Nevertheless, we may still utilize the idea of the smoothness criterion for projective toric varieties from \cite[Theorem~2.4.3]{Cox}.

    Namely, by \cite[Theorem~2.1.9]{Cox}, the vertices of the polytope $\mathscr{P}_0$ determine an affine covering 
    \[\mathcal{M}_0=\bigcup_{{\bf x}\in {\bf X}_0} \left(\mathcal{M}_0\cap \{({\bf x}) \neq 0\}\right) ,\]
    of the variety $\mathcal{M}_0$, where 
    \[{\bf X}_0:=\{{\bf x}\in\{0,1\}^k: \,v_{({\bf x})}\text{ is a vertex of $\mathscr{P}_0$}\}.\]
    Now, for each vertex $v_{({\bf x})}$ of $\mathscr{P}_0$ with ${\bf x}=(x_1,\dots,x_k)\in {\bf X}_0\subset \{0,1\}^k$ and each standard basis vector $\xi_i$ of $\mathbb R^{k_0}$ with $i\in \{1,\dots, k_0\}$, one of the two $\mathbb Z^{k_0}$-lattice points 
    $v_{({\bf x})}\pm\xi_i$ must be in $\mathscr{A}_0$. Indeed, by~\eqref{eq: exponents}, we have
    \[v_{({\bf x}+{\bf e}_i)}=v_{({\bf x})}+\xi_i \quad \text{if $x_i=0$}\]
    and 
    \[v_{({\bf x}-{\bf e}_i)}=v_{({\bf x})}-\xi_i \quad \text{if $x_i=1$}.\]
    Combinatorially, this means that the corresponding point $w_{({\bf x}),i}=v_{({\bf x})}\pm\xi_i$ is the first lattice point of $\mathscr{A}_0$ different from $v_{({\bf x})}$ encountered as one traverses the edge of $\mathscr{P}_0$ parallel to $\xi_i$ starting at $v_{({\bf x})}$. Hence, the vectors $w_{({\bf x}),i}-v_{({\bf x})}$, $i\in\{1,\dots, k_0\}$, form a basis of the $\mathbb Z^{k_0}$-lattice. It follows that the Jacobian 
     of the affine parametrization of $\mathcal{M}_0\cap \{({\bf x}) \neq 0\}$ induced by $\Psi_{0,1}$ has full rank at every point of this chart, and thus $\mathcal{M}_0\cap \{({\bf x}) \neq 0\}$ is smooth; compare the smoothness criterion from \cite[Theorem~2.4.3]{Cox}. Since these charts cover $\mathcal{M}_0$, the variety $\mathcal{M}_0$ is smooth. 
     
     Finally, the set $\mathcal{Y}=\{(\lambda, \xi): \lambda\in \mathbb{C}^*, \xi\in \mathcal{M}_0(\lambda)\}\subset \mathbb{C}^*\times \mathbb{P}^{2^k-1}$ is the image of $\mathbb{C}^*\times \mathcal{M}_0(1)$ under the biholomorphism $(\lambda,\xi)\mapsto(\lambda, D_\lambda(\xi))$, where $D_\lambda$ is the diagonal automorphism of $\mathbb{P}^{2^k-1}$ for which the $({\bf x})$-coordinate is multiplied by $\prod_{j:\; x_j=1} \lambda^{1-\#\Phi(j)}$. Hence $\mathcal{Y}$ is a complex submanifold of $\mathbb{C}^*\times \mathbb{P}^{2^k-1}$. This finishes the proof of the proposition.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- example-8: body_md -->
\label{ex: M0}\mbox{}
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
    \item\label{item: ex_M0_i} The Sierpi\'{n}ski gluing data $(H,\Phi)$ with parameters $k = m = 3$ from Example~\ref{ex: sierpinksi} is non-degenerate and pre-fixed. In fact, the induced dynamical system $\Lambda_{(H,\Phi)}$ fixes each label. It follows that the subvariety $\mathcal{M}_0=\mathcal{M}_0(\lambda)$ from Proposition~\ref{prop: pre fixed dynamics} is simply the corresponding invariant variety $\mathcal{M}\subset \mathbb{P}^{2^k-1}$ for $\mathcal{G}_k$ itself. In particular, $\mathcal{M}_0$ is smooth and $F_\lambda$ acts as the identity on the regular part of $\mathcal{M}$ for each $\lambda\in \mathbb{C}^*$.
    
    \item\label{item: ex_M0_ii} The diamond hierarchical gluing data $(H,\Phi)$ with parameters $k=2$ and $m=4$ from Example~\ref{ex: diamon_lattice} is degenerate and pre-fixed. 
    The induced dynamical system $\Lambda_{(H,\Phi)}$ sends both labels to $1$, so that $k_0 = 1$ and the subvariety $\mathcal{M}_0=\mathcal{M}_0(\lambda)$ from Proposition~\ref{prop: pre fixed dynamics} is a curve in the corresponding invariant variety $\mathcal{M}\subset \mathbb{P}^{2^k-1}$ for $\mathcal{G}_k$. In fact, on the regular part of the chart $\mathcal{M}\cap \{({\bf 0})\neq 0\}$ the renormalization map $F_\lambda$ is given by:
    \[[1:s:t:st]\mapsto [1:\frac{1}{\lambda}\,s^2:\frac{1}{\lambda}\,s^2:\frac{1}{\lambda^2}\,s^4],\]
    and thus the Zariski closure $\mathcal{M}_0$ of the image is a smooth curve. 
    

    \item\label{item: ex_M0_iii} The ($z^2+i$)-gluing data $(H,\Phi)$ with parameters $k=3$ and $m=2$ from Example~\ref{ex: dendrite} is non-degenerate but not pre-fixed. The induced dynamical system $\Lambda_{(H,\Phi)}$ interchanges the labels $2$ and $3$, while the label $1$ is pre-periodic. Thus the regular part of the $3$-dimensional variety $\mathcal{M} \subset \mathbb{P}^7$ is mapped onto the smooth $2$-dimensional subvariety $\mathcal{M}_0$, and regular points of $\mathcal{M}_0$ are fixed under the action of the second iterate $F_\lambda^2$.
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end example-8 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
For degenerate gluing data, the subvariety $\mathcal{M}_0$ from Proposition~\ref{prop: pre fixed dynamics} may or may not be smooth. We consider two examples, both for $k=2$ marked vertices. We will only provide the map $\Lambda=\Lambda_{(H,\Phi)}$ on labels and their local degrees, leaving it to the reader to supply the corresponding gluing data $(H,\Phi)$. In both cases, we take $\Lambda(1) = \Lambda(2) = 1$, so that $k_0 = 1$ and $\mathcal{M}_0$ is a curve in $\mathcal{M}\subset \mathbb{P}^3$, and we let $\# \Phi(1) = 2$, so that the gluing data $(H,\Phi)$ is degenerate.  

When $\# \Phi(2) = 1$, the renormalization map $F_\lambda$ defined on the regular part of the chart $\mathcal{M}\cap \{({\bf 0}) \neq 0\}$ is given by 
\[
[1:s:t:st] \mapsto [1:\frac{1}{\lambda}s^2 : s: \frac{1}{\lambda}s^3], 
\]
and hence the Zariski closure $\mathcal{M}_0$ of the image is a smooth curve. (See also Example~\ref{ex: M0}\ref{item: ex_M0_ii} for the case when $\#\Phi(2)=2$, in which case $\mathcal{M}_0$ is also a smooth curve.) However, when $\# \Phi(2) = 3$, the respective map $F_\lambda$ on the regular
part of the same chart of $\mathcal{M}$ is given by
\[
[1:s:t:st] \mapsto [1 : \frac{1}{\lambda}s^2 : \frac{1}{\lambda^2}s^3 : \frac{1}{\lambda^3}s^5].
\]
In this case, the Zariski closure $\mathcal{M}_0$ of the image is a curve with cusp singularities at $[1:0:0:0]$ and $[0:0:0:1]$.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- definition-9: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{X}$ be a smooth manifold and $\mathcal{Y} \subset \mathcal{X}$ be a smooth submanifold. Suppose $F:U(\mathcal{Y})\to \mathcal{X}$ is a smooth map defined on a neighborhood $U(\mathcal{Y})$ of $\mathcal{Y}$ that fixes the submanifold $\mathcal{Y}$ pointwise. We say that $F$ is \emph{transversally superattracting} on $\mathcal{Y}$ (or simply that $\mathcal{Y}$ is transversally superattracting, when $F$ is understood) if at every point $p \in \mathcal{Y}$, the characteristic polynomial $\det(z\cdot \mathrm{Id}-DF(p))$ of the differential $DF(p)$ has the form $(z-1)^{\dim \mathcal{Y}}z^{\dim\mathcal{X} - \dim\mathcal{Y}}$.
<!-- end definition-9 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{lem: trans_superattr}
     Let $\mathcal{X}$ be a smooth manifold, $\mathcal{Y}\subset \mathcal{X}$ be a smooth submanifold, and let $F:U(\mathcal{Y})\to \mathcal{X}$ be a smooth map defined on a neighborhood $U(\mathcal{Y})$ of $\mathcal{Y}$ that fixes the submanifold $\mathcal{Y}$ pointwise. 
     
     Suppose $F$ is transversally superattracting on $\mathcal{Y}$, and consider an arbitrary point $p_0 \in \mathcal{Y}$ and a coordinate neighborhood $U(p_0)$. Then there exists a sub-neighborhood $U'(p_0)\subset U(p_0)$ such that for every starting point $p\in U'(p_0)$, the orbit $(F^n(p))_{n\geq 0}$ is well-defined, remains in $U(p_0)$, and converges to a point $p_\infty=p_\infty(p)\in\mathcal{Y}$ superexponentially fast with respect to the Euclidean metric on $U(p_0)$. 

     Conversely, the map $F$ is transversally superattracting on $\mathcal{Y}$ if the following holds for each point $p_0\in \mathcal{Y}$: there exists some coordinate neighborhood $U(p_0)$ of $p_0$ and a sub-neighborhood $U'(p_0)\subset U(p_0)$ such that the orbits of points $p\in U'(p_0)$ are well-defined, 
     remain in $U(p_0)$, and converge to $\mathcal{Y}$ superexponentially fast with respect to the Euclidean metric on $U(p_0)$.
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm: superattraction}
    Suppose that a recursion operator $\mathcal{R}$ on $\mathcal{G}_k$ with $k\geq 1$ is non-degenerate, pre-fixed, and expanding, and fix $\lambda\in \mathbb{C}^*$.  Then the renormalization map $F_\lambda: \mathbb{P}^{2^k-1}\dashrightarrow \mathbb{P}^{2^k-1}$ is transversally superattracting on $\mathcal{M}_0=\mathcal{M}_0(\lambda)$.
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- prop-3: body_md -->
\label{prop: attracting for graphs}
     Let $(H,\Phi)$ be a gluing data with parameters $k\geq 2, m \geq 2$ that satisfies the following condition: 
    \begin{enumerate}[label=($\star$),font=\normalfont]
        \item\label{cond: superattraction} for each pair $j,j'\in \{1,\dots, k\}$ of distinct labels the corresponding edges $\Phi(j)$ and $\Phi(j')$ of   the gluing scheme $H$ form disjoint subsets of $V(H)$.
    \end{enumerate}
    Let $\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{(H,\Phi)}$, and fix $\lambda\in \mathbb R_+$ and $\Delta\in \mathbb N$. Then there are constants $\varepsilon_0 >0$ and $C>0$ such that for any graph $G\in \mathcal{G}_k$ whose marked vertices are pairwise non-adjacent and have degrees bounded by $\Delta$ the following implication holds: if $\theta_\lambda(G)\leq \varepsilon$ for some $0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_0$, then $\theta_\lambda(\mathcal{R}(G))\leq C \varepsilon^2$.
<!-- end prop-3 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lemma: effect of gluing}
    Fix $\lambda\in\mathbb R_+$ and $\Delta, k, m\in \mathbb N$. Then there are constants $\varepsilon_0 > 0$ and $C_0>0$ such that the following holds for any recursion operator $\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{(H,\Phi)}$ with parameters $k$ and $m'\leq m$ and a graph $G\in \mathcal{G}_k$ whose marked vertices are non-adjacent and have degrees bounded by $\Delta$:

    If $\theta_\lambda(G)\leq\varepsilon$ for some $0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_0$, then 
    \[
    \theta^S_\lambda(\mathcal{R}(G))=\max_{\tau, \sigma} |\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{R}(G),\lambda}[\tau:\sigma] - \mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{R}(G),\lambda}[\tau]| \leq C_0 \varepsilon, 
    \]
    where the maximum is taken over all assignments $\tau$ and $\sigma$ on disjoint subsets of the set $S$ of vertices of $\mathcal{R}(G)$ induced by $E(H)$.
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
The statement of the lemma may be proven directly using explicit computations of conditional probabilities. However, we provide instead a more elegant argument which relies on a natural extension of the material discussed in Sections~\ref{subsec: induced dynamics}--\ref{subsec: dynamics on variety}.   

For each $\lambda\in \mathbb{C}^*$, we may partition the independence polynomial $Z_{\mathcal{R}(G)}(\lambda)$ according to the vertex assignments $\eta$ on the set $S$. Arguing as in Section~\ref{subsec: induced dynamics}, the recursion operator $\mathcal{R}$ then induces a rational map $F^S_\lambda: \mathbb{P}^{2^k-1}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2^s-1}$, with $s=\#S$, where we use the coordinates on $\mathbb{P}^{2^s-1}$ corresponding to the $\eta$-conditioned independence polynomials $Z_{\mathcal{R}(G)}^\eta(\lambda)$. Similarly to Section~\ref{subsec: invariant variety}, we may introduce a subvariety $\mathcal{M}^S$ of $\mathbb{P}^{2^s-1}$ defined by the absence of correlations between the vertices in $S$ for $\mathcal{R}(G)$. Proposition~\ref{prop: invariance} then naturally generalizes to the statement $F^S_\lambda(\mathcal{M})\subset \mathcal{M}^S$.
